Now I have the registry data. Let me verify the key numbers:

- **78.9%**: 75/95 non-tie labels from death asymmetry. From plan-013: det_preferred_death_asymmetry=70, but also int_preferred_death_asymmetry (likely 5). So death asymmetry labels = 70+5 = 75. Non-tie labels = 86+9 = 95. 75/95 = 78.9%. ✓
- **DAF = 90.4%**: plan-012-full: death_avoidance_fraction=0.9041 → 90.4%. ✓


- **δ = 0.2653**: From plan-012-penalty-sensitivity, and when I calculate the predicted DAF at P=100 using the formula δP/(δP + Δ_nd), I get 26.53/29.37 = 90.3%, which matches the observed 90.4% closely. ✓
- **r = 0.97**: The correlation coefficient isn't directly listed in plan-012-full, but the p-value of 8.12e-07 indicates a highly significant relationship.
- **245 episodes**: Confirmed from plan-012-full. ✓
- **MATH**: The extension with n=795 shows internal accuracy at 73.46% and code accuracy at 72.96%, with p=0.8103. ✓
- **ALFWorld**: 135 samples across 6 task types. ✓
- **Llama**: 70 episodes with 7 tasks, where 24 showed deterministic preference and none showed interpretability preference. ✓
- **4B e2e filtered**: The score of -12.03 with confidence interval [-17.82, -6.45] is what the Director wants me to use instead of the unfiltered version. ✓
- **SCI**: 0.2147 with top-3 performance at 39.8%. ✓

Now I need to execute the Director's comprehensive instructions.

I'm planning a major restructuring in two phases. First, I'll reorganize the narrative to make 78.9% the primary finding while positioning DAF=90.4% as secondary context, then add a predictability analysis showing how δ=0.265 and P=100 predict the observed 90.4%, with r=0.97 serving as a consistency validation. Second, I'll reorganize the content by moving the SCI section to the appendix to free up space, bringing the per-task table from Appendix B into the main text, integrating the penalty sensitivity findings into the main discussion, and expanding the Discussion section.

The challenge is staying within the 8-page limit while making these additions. Moving SCI out saves roughly 0.3-0.5 pages, but bringing in the per-task table uses 0.3 pages, adding penalty sensitivity findings uses 0.1 page, and expanding Discussion uses 0.5 pages. I need to cut the SCI formal definitions and Gini coefficient equations to the appendix, keep the per-task table concise, distill the penalty sensitivity into 1-2 sentences, and carefully manage the Discussion expansion to balance everything out.

Now I'm working through the remaining revisions: downgrading C3 to preliminary status and removing the framework boundary conditions claim, adding qualifiers to Llama and ALFWorld results, strengthening the K=11 caveat in the statistical section, noting episode independence in Limitations, adding confidence intervals to the per-task table, fixing terminology from "data scaling" to "augmentation scaling," correcting the 4B filtered score, addressing the mislabeling fraction, reducing the abstract numbers, and gathering new citations. Finally, I need to verify the MATH framing doesn't overstate anything.

For formatting, I'll handle title capitalization, reference formatting, and the Responsible NLP Checklist. Given the scope of these changes, I'm prioritizing the structural moves first—shifting SCI to the appendix and relocating the per-task content—since those free up the most space. Then I'll execute the narrative restructuring across all sections, which is the highest priority change, before moving into the smaller fixes and formatting details. I need to search for those new citations once I get to that phase.

Actually, I just realized the Director specified that the 4B model should use filtered 245-episode data instead of the unfiltered 275 episodes, which shifts the e2e score from -10.72 to -12.03 and updates the confidence interval accordingly. I need to make sure that change propagates through the experiments section and any related tables.

There's also a note about the 1.7B model potentially overfitting to death-prediction correlations during training, which might explain the performance dip. And I should swap "data scaling" for "augmentation scaling" throughout the document.

Let me tackle the structural changes first. I'm going to move the SCI subsection from the methods section into the appendix, then replace it with a brief descriptive sentence in the original location. After that, I'll work through the N1 narrative restructuring, starting with the abstract.

```text
107	
108	\subsection{Signal Concentration Index}
109	\label{sec:sci}
110	
111	DAF quantifies \emph{how much} routing signal derives from death avoidance; a complementary question is whether this signal is driven by a few outlier task types or distributed systematically. We define the Signal Concentration Index (SCI) using the Gini coefficient over per-task routing signal contributions.
112	
113	Let $K$ denote the number of task types and $c_k$ the contribution of task type $k$ to the aggregate routing signal, defined as the fraction of total death-rate differential attributable to task $k$:
114	\begin{equation}
115	    c_k = \frac{n_k \cdot \delta_k}{\sum_{j=1}^{K} n_j \cdot \delta_j}
116	\end{equation}
117	where $n_k$ is the number of episodes and $\delta_k = d_k^{\tt int} - d_k^{\tt det}$ is the per-task death-rate differential. The SCI is the Gini coefficient over $\{c_1, \ldots, c_K\}$:
118	\begin{equation}
119	    \text{SCI} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^K \sum_{j=1}^K |c_i - c_j|}{2K \sum_{k=1}^K c_k}
120	\end{equation}
121	
122	SCI $\in [0, 1]$: values near 0 indicate uniform distribution across task types (systematic phenomenon); values near 1 indicate concentration in a single type (isolated phenomenon). Together with DAF, SCI determines whether death avoidance is both dominant \emph{and} systematic. Results are reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
123	
```

</details>

43	\appendix
44	
45	\section{Labeling Quality Audit}
46	\label{app:labeling}
47	
48	A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \texttt{no\_preference} labels (61.2\%), 115 (46.9\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\%) are both-dead, all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\%, within 1$\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\pm$ 0.5\%, step-level Trainer evaluation); score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 97.4\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.
49	
50	\section{Per-Task Decomposition}
51	\label{app:per-task}
52	We select 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types via prescreening: task types where both strategies complete at least one episode within the 30-step budget are retained; types where both strategies time out on all seed variations are excluded.\footnote{Prescreening ensures that the counterfactual comparison is meaningful: task types where neither strategy makes progress provide no routing signal.}
53	
54	\begin{table}[h]
55	\centering
56	\scriptsize
57	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{2.5pt}

{

  "old_string": "\\section{Labeling Quality Audit}\n\\label{app:labeling}",
  "new_string": "\\section{Signal Concentration Index}\n\\label{app:sci}\n\nLet $K$ denote the number of task types and $c_k$ the contribution of task type $k$ to the aggregate routing signal, defined as the fraction of total death-rate differential attributable to task $k$:\n\\begin{equation}\n    c_k = \\frac{n_k \\cdot \\delta_k}{\\sum_{j=1}^{K} n_j \\cdot \\delta_j}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $n_k$ is the number of episodes and $\\delta_k = d_k^{\\tt int} - d_k^{\\tt det}$ is the per-task death-rate differential. The SCI is the Gini coefficient over $\\{c_1, \\ldots, c_K\\}$:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{SCI} = \\frac{\\sum_{i=1}^K \\sum_{j=1}^K |c_i - c_j|}{2K \\sum_{k=1}^K c_k}\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\section{Labeling Quality Audit}\n\\label{app:labeling}"
}
```

19	The SCI (\S\ref{sec:sci}) over per-task routing signal contributions is $0.2147$ (low concentration): the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\%$ of total death-rate differential. One outlier, \texttt{find-non-living-thing}, has zero deaths (DAF $= 0\%$, $\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores); all 10 remaining types exhibit DAF $= 100\%$. Death avoidance is systematic across all task types exhibiting routing signal (11/30 after prescreening), not driven by a few outlier tasks.
20	
21	\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.}

          " The internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = P \\cdot \\delta + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ makes $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\\delta$ with residual $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \\rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \\leq 25$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).           " Episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$) yields a 95\\% CI of $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for the aggregate DAF, excluding both 50\\% and 100\\%. This CI quantifies within-sample uncertainty; generalization to unseen task types requires held-out evaluation. Per-task CIs confirm $\\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types; \\texttt{chemistry-mix} ($N{=}20$, CI $[-5.0, 48.6]$) is the sole exception, attributable to small sample size. As a sensitivity analysis accounting for within-task-type correlation, cluster bootstrap resampling ($K{=}11$ task-type clusters, $B = 10{,}000$) yields a wider 95\\% CI $[73.3\\%, 100\\%]$. With $K{=}11$ clusters, this interval should be interpreted cautiously: \\citet{cameron-2008} recommend $K \\geq 40$ for reliable cluster bootstrap inference. {
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
  "old_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nModular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \\emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework using penalty-independent and penalty-dependent decomposition that isolates death-avoidance from non-death routing signal, requiring only paired episode outcomes with binary success/failure indicators. Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), the framework reveals that \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} (75 of 95) arise from death asymmetry (penalty-independent); the penalty-weighted DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$. The per-task $r{=}0.97$ follows algebraically from death dominance when $P$ is constant. A router trained on these labels reaches only $59.0\\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy at 4B parameters, failing to outperform always-deterministic. Cross-environment contrasts confirm framework predictions: on MATH ($N{=}795$), no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($p = 0.81$); on ALFWorld ($N{=}135$), the framework identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct pilot confirms directional consistency across model families. We predict that stronger backbones will lower death-avoidance fractions as capability reduces catastrophic failure rates.\n\\end{abstract}",
  "new_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nModular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \\emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that isolates death-avoidance from non-death routing signal using both penalty-independent label counting and a penalty-dependent decomposition (DAF). Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} (75 of 95) arise from death asymmetry, a penalty-independent finding. Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\\%$, consistent with the predicted value from observed death-rate differential $\\delta{=}0.265$. A per-task consistency check yields $r{=}0.97$, confirming death dominance. A router trained on these labels reaches only $59.0\\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy at 4B parameters, not outperforming always-deterministic. Cross-environment contrasts confirm framework predictions: on MATH ($N{=}795$), no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($p = 0.81$); on ALFWorld ($N{=}135$), the framework identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism. We predict that stronger backbones will lower death-avoidance fractions as capability reduces catastrophic failure rates.\n\\end{abstract}"
}

A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut this assumption has never been systematically verified: \\emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}\nIf routing value traces to a single failure mode, practitioners can replace complex learned routers with simpler safety-aware policies at lower computational cost and deployment risk.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{formal diagnostic framework} for answering this question. The framework comprises (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), a metric that decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, the framework reveals a surprisingly concentrated answer: \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95, penalty-independent); under ScienceWorld's default penalty ($P{=}100$), this corresponds to DAF $= 90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$).\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap between strategies collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$. Per-task, the death-rate differential correlates almost perfectly with the performance gap ($r = 0.97$), a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nThis framework addresses a methodological gap.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels, per-step oracle comparison, degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, so no step-level signal exists (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nOur counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}). The framework is applicable to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes, requiring only paired episode outcomes under two modalities.\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$).\nWithout death risk, modality routing confers no systematic advantage.\nSecond, a cross-model replication with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, 70 episode pairs) shows direction-consistent death patterns for all task types, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}); the phenomenon generalizes across model families.\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 with LoRA, 0.6B--4B) trained on these counterfactual labels yields flat step-level balanced accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0\\%$), while smaller scales remain at or below chance. Data scaling ($32{\\times}$) ablations confirm this is structural rather than data-limited (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{A formal episode-level counterfactual framework (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}) with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics} for decomposing routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. The framework applies to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical application to ScienceWorld} revealing that 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels are driven by death asymmetry (penalty-independent), with DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (penalty-dependent). Router training yields flat step-level accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the baseline ($59.0\\%$), and the cost is disproportionate to routing benefit. Death dominance renders always-deterministic near-optimal (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-environment diagnostic contrasts} establishing framework boundary conditions: MATH ($N{=}795$) confirms the predicted null ($p = 0.81$, no routing signal without catastrophic failure); ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) identifies an alternative mechanism (information asymmetry); a Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct pilot confirms directional consistency across model families. True quantitative DAF replication requires a second catastrophic-failure environment (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\n",

     ```latex
     \begin{abstract}
     Modular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that isolates death-avoidance from non-death routing signal using both penalty-independent label counting and a penalty-dependent decomposition (DAF). Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} (75 of 95) arise from death asymmetry, a penalty-independent finding. Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\%$, consistent with the predicted value from observed death-rate differential $\delta{=}0.265$. A per-task consistency check yields $r{=}0.97$, confirming death dominance. A router trained on these labels reaches only $59.0\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy at 4B parameters, not outperforming always-deterministic. Cross-environment contrasts confirm framework predictions: on MATH ($N{=}795$), no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($p = 0.81$); on ALFWorld ($N{=}135$), the framework identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism. We predict that stronger backbones will lower death-avoidance fractions as capability reduces catastrophic failure rates.
     \end{abstract}
     ```

        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut this assumption has never been systematically verified: \\emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}\nIf routing value traces to a single failure mode, practitioners can replace complex learned routers with simpler safety-aware policies at lower computational cost and deployment risk.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{formal diagnostic framework} for answering this question. The framework comprises (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), a penalty-dependent metric that decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding that holds regardless of how death is penalized. Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$), consistent with the predicted value from observed $\\delta{=}0.265$ and $P{=}100$.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nThis framework addresses a methodological gap.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels, per-step oracle comparison, degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, so no step-level signal exists (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nOur counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}). The framework is applicable to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes, requiring only paired episode outcomes under two modalities.\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$).\nWithout death risk, modality routing confers no systematic advantage.\nSecond, a cross-model replication with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, 70 episode pairs) shows direction-consistent death patterns for all task types, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}); the phenomenon generalizes across model families.\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 with LoRA, 0.6B--4B) trained on these counterfactual labels yields flat step-level balanced accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0\\%$), while smaller scales remain at or below chance. Data scaling ($32{\\times}$) ablations confirm this is structural rather than data-limited (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{A formal episode-level counterfactual framework (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}) with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics} for decomposing routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. The framework applies to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical application to ScienceWorld} revealing that 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels are driven by death asymmetry (penalty-independent), with DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (penalty-dependent). Router training yields flat step-level accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the baseline ($59.0\\%$), and the cost is disproportionate to routing benefit. Death dominance renders always-deterministic near-optimal (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-environment diagnostic contrasts} establishing framework boundary conditions: MATH ($N{=}795$) confirms the predicted null ($p = 0.81$, no routing signal without catastrophic failure); ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) identifies an alternative mechanism (information asymmetry); a Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct pilot confirms directional consistency across model families. True quantitative DAF replication requires a second catastrophic-failure environment (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\n",
        "numLines": 39,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 39
      }

      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:death-decomp}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\\ performance gap ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types.}\n\\label{fig:decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross 245 episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$; at default $P{=}100$). The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction is 78.9\\% (75 of 95 non-tie labels). Of these 75, 54 (72.0\\%) reduce to ties when the death penalty is removed entirely: in those episodes, both modalities score zero without death, so the only reason one strategy outscored the other was avoiding the $-100$ penalty. Death avoidance is not merely the dominant but often the \\emph{sole} discriminating signal between modalities. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score, but the death penalty still accounts for the majority of the score gap.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}\nThe internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = P \\cdot \\delta + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ makes $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\\delta$ with residual $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \\rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \\leq 25$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 9 \\texttt{internal\\_preferred}, and 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference}.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal concentration.}\nThe Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\\%$ of total death-rate differential. One outlier, \\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, has zero deaths ($\\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores alone); all 10 remaining types exhibit death-asymmetry-driven routing labels. Death avoidance is systematic across all task types exhibiting routing signal (11/30 after prescreening), not driven by a few outlier tasks.\n\n\\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.}\nEpisode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$) yields a 95\\% CI of $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for the aggregate DAF, excluding both 50\\% and 100\\%. This CI quantifies within-sample uncertainty; generalization to unseen task types requires held-out evaluation. Per-task CIs confirm $\\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types; \\texttt{chemistry-mix} ($N{=}20$, CI $[-5.0, 48.6]$) is the sole exception, attributable to small sample size. As a sensitivity analysis accounting for within-task-type correlation, cluster bootstrap resampling ($K{=}11$ task-type clusters, $B = 10{,}000$) yields a wider 95\\% CI $[73.3\\%, 100\\%]$. With $K{=}11$ clusters, this interval should be interpreted cautiously: \\citet{cameron-2008} recommend $K \\geq 40$ for reliable cluster bootstrap inference. The lower bound nonetheless remains well above 50\\%.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nA Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\\citep{llama3-2024} replication on 7 task types ($N{=}70$) shows the death-avoidance pattern reproduces and intensifies: internal death rate $34.3\\%$ vs.\\ deterministic $0\\%$ (cf.\\ Qwen: $42.9\\%$ vs.\\ $16.3\\%$), with zero internal-preferred episodes (24 det-preferred, 46 no-preference). With $N{=}70$ and zero internal-preferred labels, this preliminary pilot demonstrates directional consistency of the death mechanism across model families, not full framework replication, which would require a larger sample and independent environment. Both models are in the 7--8B range; 70B-class models and Reflexion-augmented reasoning represent testable predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Domain Negative Contrast: MATH}\n\\label{sec:math-contrast}\n\nOn MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy: $73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$ ($+0.5$~pp, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp). The McNemar test compares binary correctness: 80 problems are solved only by internal reasoning and 76 only by code, yielding near-symmetric discordance ($\\chi^2 = 0.10$). The label distribution is balanced: 107 \\texttt{int\\_preferred} vs.\\ 95 \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, with 74.6\\% \\texttt{no\\_preference}. The confirmed null rules out any routing signal of magnitude ${\\geq}3.6$~pp at $\\alpha{=}0.05$. The absence of systematic routing signal confirms that without catastrophic failure, modality routing confers no advantage in this domain.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment Contrasts}\n\\label{sec:cross-env}\n\nThe framework generates testable predictions beyond ScienceWorld. We evaluate two environments that lack catastrophic death penalties, testing whether the death-avoidance mechanism is specific to environments with binary catastrophic failure.\n\n\\paragraph{ALFWorld.} We apply the framework to ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021}, a text-based household environment (6 task types, 135 games, max 50 steps, binary success metric). Unlike ScienceWorld, ALFWorld has no death penalty; failure means exhausting the step budget. The two modalities differ in \\emph{information access}: the internal strategy receives only free-text observations, while the deterministic strategy additionally receives admissible actions at each step (an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch). The success rate gap is large: 27.4\\% (deterministic) vs.\\ 3.0\\% (internal), reflecting the 7B model's near-inability to solve household tasks without admissible-action guidance. The label distribution shows 35 deterministic-preferred (25.9\\%), 2 internal-preferred (1.5\\%), and 98 no-preference (72.6\\%), of which 96 are both-fail. The deterministic advantage stems entirely from the information gap rather than death avoidance, a qualitatively different routing mechanism that the framework diagnoses without modification.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nEnvironment & $N$ & Det\\% & Int\\% & NoPref\\% & Death? \\\\\n\\midrule\nScienceWorld & 245 & 35.1 & 3.7 & 61.2 & Yes ($-100$) \\\\\nMATH & 795 & 12.0 & 13.5 & 74.6 & No \\\\\nALFWorld & 135 & 25.9 & 1.5 & 72.6 & No\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Cross-environment label distribution. ScienceWorld exhibits strong deterministic preference driven by death avoidance; MATH shows balanced labels (no routing signal); ALFWorld shows deterministic preference driven by information asymmetry. $\\dagger$\\,ALFWorld's deterministic advantage stems from action-space pruning, not death avoidance.}\n\\label{tab:cross-env}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}\n\\caption{Label distribution across environments. In ScienceWorld (with $-100$ death penalty), deterministic-preferred labels dominate (86:9). In MATH (no death penalty, $N{=}795$), labels are balanced (95:107), with 74.6\\% no preference.}\n\\label{fig:signal-reversal}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Summary.} Table~\\ref{tab:cross-env} and Figure~\\ref{fig:signal-reversal} summarize the three environments. The contrast is systematic: ScienceWorld's death penalty produces strongly asymmetric labels (86:9); MATH's absence of catastrophic failure produces balanced labels (95:107); ALFWorld's information gap produces moderately asymmetric labels (35:2) through a distinct mechanism. These results establish boundary conditions for the death-avoidance finding rather than claiming its universality.\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 tasks, 245 episodes). $\\dagger$\\,Single seed; see \\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nAlways-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic, an \\emph{oracle degeneracy} indicating the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.\n\n\\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA fine-tuned; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) is trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with identical 4-seed configurations. Step-level balanced accuracy is flat across scales ($56.4 \\pm 3.5\\%$, $58.2 \\pm 4.0\\%$, $59.2 \\pm 3.5\\%$; all within seed variance); step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}), while 0.6B ($50.7 \\pm 2.6\\%$) and 1.7B ($43.1 \\pm 1.0\\%$) remain at or below chance. The 1.7B dip is robust across seeds (range $41.7$--$44.4\\%$); this non-monotonic pattern, combined with universally low internal recall, reinforces that learned routing does not scale predictably with capacity at current data scales.\n\n\\paragraph{Data scaling.} Augmentation-based data scaling ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) provides no improvement (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}), but paraphrase augmentation inflates the majority class (89.6\\%$\\rightarrow$97.7\\%) without generating novel routing patterns; real-data scaling (requiring ${\\sim}1{,}350$ episode pairs for 50 minority-class examples) remains an open question. Capacity scaling improves episode-level routing ($+9$\\,pp at 4B) without improving step-level accuracy (flat ${\\sim}57\\%$); the extractable signal is task-type-level structure.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} We deploy the best router (4B) in the full ScienceWorld loop ($N{=}275$ episodes)\\footnote{The 4B evaluation uses $N{=}275$ episodes (vs.\\ $N{=}245$ for baselines in Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}) due to expanded task coverage during data collection. Score distributions are comparable across the shared task types.} to test whether improved episode-level accuracy translates to deployment gains. The 4B router scores $-10.72$ (95\\% CI: $[-15.69, -5.52]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). The 1.7B router ($-10.70$; Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}) performs comparably. Despite improved episode-level accuracy ($+9$\\,pp at 4B), deployment score remains flat at ${\\sim}{-10.7}$: the DAF decomposition identifies this ceiling without requiring expensive router training at multiple scales. Label quality is verified in Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}.\n\nFailure mode and ablation details in Appendices~\\ref{app:failure-modes}--\\ref{app:ablations}.\n",
        "numLines": 102,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 102
      }
    },

{
  "parentUuid": "9d84e68b-eebc-48ea-bf4e-521d6071cf7b",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically across the environment (\\S\\ref{sec:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment $\\mathcal{E}$ with state space $\\mathcal{S}$, observation space $\\mathcal{O}$, and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent follows a policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ that maps observation histories to actions. At each step $t$, the agent receives observation $o_t$, selects action $a_t = \\pi(o_1, \\ldots, o_t)$, and the environment transitions to state $s_{t+1}$.\n\nWe define a modality set $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$ governing how the agent processes state transitions:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Internal} ($m = {\\tt int}$): The agent uses its own generation to predict intermediate observations. The LLM interleaves chain-of-thought reasoning with environment actions following the ReAct paradigm \\cite{react-2023}, but prediction errors compound across steps.\n    \\item \\textbf{Deterministic} ($m = {\\tt det}$): The agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step, equivalent to access to a perfect dynamics model. Action selection remains with the LLM; only the state-transition prediction is replaced.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent We restrict $\\mathcal{M}$ to two modalities. A reasoning-depth ablation (chain-of-thought vs.\\ direct-answer, $n{=}120$, 6 task types) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, near-identical death rates); details in Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}.\n\nA \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which modality to use at each step. The goal of modality routing is to learn $\\rho$ that maximizes expected episode return. Our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn such a router.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality, yielding no routing signal. We instead perform episode-level counterfactual analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and modality $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the modality choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two modalities can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score to reflect the asymmetric cost of catastrophic failure in interactive environments. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. The full procedure is given in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, modalities $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nThe algorithm prioritizes survival over score: death asymmetry (lines 5--8) determines preference before score comparison (lines 11--14). Label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. The full gap decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation holds exactly when the surviving strategy scores zero in all death-asymmetry episodes; in our data, the residual is small ($29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$, or $0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nA complementary question is whether death-avoidance signal is driven by a few outlier task types or distributed systematically. We quantify this via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI) based on the Gini coefficient over per-task routing signal contributions (formal definition in Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). SCI $\\in [0, 1]$: values near 0 indicate uniform distribution (systematic phenomenon); values near 1 indicate concentration in a single type (isolated phenomenon). Results are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n",
        "numLines": 109,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 109
      }
    },

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We presented a formal diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents via episode-level counterfactual analysis with dual metrics (penalty-independent label fraction and penalty-dependent DAF). Applied to 245 ScienceWorld episodes (11 of 30 task types, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), death asymmetry accounts for 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels; DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$). Cross-domain contrasts confirm framework predictions: MATH shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure; ALFWorld reveals information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism; a Llama pilot confirms cross-model consistency. Open directions include replication in catastrophic-failure environments (e.g., NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020}, Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022}) and stronger backbones, which the framework predicts will lower DAF.
4	
```

_source line 3199 · 2026-05-17T14:56:32.459Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013eBTd9eGfZ2nyVcBkhD3Jr`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
4	\label{sec:death-decomp}
5	
6	\begin{figure}[t]
7	\centering
8	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}
9	\caption{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\ performance gap ($\Delta_{\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types.}
10	\label{fig:decomposition}
11	\end{figure}
12	
13	Across 245 episodes, $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$; at default $P{=}100$). The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction is 78.9\% (75 of 95 non-tie labels). Of these 75, 54 (72.0\%) reduce to ties when the death penalty is removed entirely: in those episodes, both modalities score zero without death, so the only reason one strategy outscored the other was avoiding the $-100$ penalty. Death avoidance is not merely the dominant but often the \emph{sole} discriminating signal between modalities. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score, but the death penalty still accounts for the majority of the score gap.
14	
15	\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}
16	The internal strategy dies in 42.9\% of episodes versus 16.3\% for deterministic (2.6$\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\Delta_{\text{full}} = P \cdot \delta + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$ makes $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\delta$ with residual $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \leq 25$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \texttt{det\_preferred}, 9 \texttt{internal\_preferred}, and 150 \texttt{no\_preference}.
17	
18	\paragraph{Signal concentration.}
19	The Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\%$ of total death-rate differential. One outlier, \texttt{find-non-living-thing}, has zero deaths ($\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores alone); all 10 remaining types exhibit death-asymmetry-driven routing labels. Death avoidance is systematic across all task types exhibiting routing signal (11/30 after prescreening), not driven by a few outlier tasks.
20	
21	\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.}
22	Episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$) yields a 95\% CI of $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$ for the aggregate DAF, excluding both 50\% and 100\%. This CI quantifies within-sample uncertainty; generalization to unseen task types requires held-out evaluation. Per-task CIs confirm $\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types; \texttt{chemistry-mix} ($N{=}20$, CI $[-5.0, 48.6]$) is the sole exception, attributable to small sample size. As a sensitivity analysis accounting for within-task-type correlation, cluster bootstrap resampling ($K{=}11$ task-type clusters, $B = 10{,}000$) yields a wider 95\% CI $[73.3\%, 100\%]$. With $K{=}11$ clusters, this interval should be interpreted cautiously: \citet{cameron-2008} recommend $K \geq 40$ for reliable cluster bootstrap inference. The lower bound nonetheless remains well above 50\%.
23	
24	\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}
25	\label{sec:cross-model}
26	
27	A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\citep{llama3-2024} replication on 7 task types ($N{=}70$) shows the death-avoidance pattern reproduces and intensifies: internal death rate $34.3\%$ vs.\ deterministic $0\%$ (cf.\ Qwen: $42.9\%$ vs.\ $16.3\%$), with zero internal-preferred episodes (24 det-preferred, 46 no-preference). With $N{=}70$ and zero internal-preferred labels, this preliminary pilot demonstrates directional consistency of the death mechanism across model families, not full framework replication, which would require a larger sample and independent environment. Both models are in the 7--8B range; 70B-class models and Reflexion-augmented reasoning represent testable predictions (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
28	
29	\subsection{Cross-Domain Negative Contrast: MATH}
30	\label{sec:math-contrast}
31	
32	On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy: $73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$ ($+0.5$~pp, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp). The McNemar test compares binary correctness: 80 problems are solved only by internal reasoning and 76 only by code, yielding near-symmetric discordance ($\chi^2 = 0.10$). The label distribution is balanced: 107 \texttt{int\_preferred} vs.\ 95 \texttt{det\_preferred}, with 74.6\% \texttt{no\_preference}. The confirmed null rules out any routing signal of magnitude ${\geq}3.6$~pp at $\alpha{=}0.05$. The absence of systematic routing signal confirms that without catastrophic failure, modality routing confers no advantage in this domain.
33	
34	\subsection{Cross-Environment Contrasts}
35	\label{sec:cross-env}
36	
37	The framework generates testable predictions beyond ScienceWorld. We evaluate two environments that lack catastrophic death penalties, testing whether the death-avoidance mechanism is specific to environments with binary catastrophic failure.
38	
39	\paragraph{ALFWorld.} We apply the framework to ALFWorld~\citep{alfworld-2021}, a text-based household environment (6 task types, 135 games, max 50 steps, binary success metric). Unlike ScienceWorld, ALFWorld has no death penalty; failure means exhausting the step budget. The two modalities differ in \emph{information access}: the internal strategy receives only free-text observations, while the deterministic strategy additionally receives admissible actions at each step (an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch). The success rate gap is large: 27.4\% (deterministic) vs.\ 3.0\% (internal), reflecting the 7B model's near-inability to solve household tasks without admissible-action guidance. The label distribution shows 35 deterministic-preferred (25.9\%), 2 internal-preferred (1.5\%), and 98 no-preference (72.6\%), of which 96 are both-fail. The deterministic advantage stems entirely from the information gap rather than death avoidance, a qualitatively different routing mechanism that the framework diagnoses without modification.
40	
41	\begin{table}[t]
42	\centering
43	\small
44	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
45	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
46	\toprule
47	Environment & $N$ & Det\% & Int\% & NoPref\% & Death? \\
48	\midrule
49	ScienceWorld & 245 & 35.1 & 3.7 & 61.2 & Yes ($-100$) \\
50	MATH & 795 & 12.0 & 13.5 & 74.6 & No \\
51	ALFWorld & 135 & 25.9 & 1.5 & 72.6 & No\textsuperscript{$\dagger$} \\
52	\bottomrule
53	\end{tabular}}
54	\caption{Cross-environment label distribution. ScienceWorld exhibits strong deterministic preference driven by death avoidance; MATH shows balanced labels (no routing signal); ALFWorld shows deterministic preference driven by information asymmetry. $\dagger$\,ALFWorld's deterministic advantage stems from action-space pruning, not death avoidance.}
55	\label{tab:cross-env}
56	\end{table}
57	
58	\begin{figure}[t]
59	\centering
60	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}
61	\caption{Label distribution across environments. In ScienceWorld (with $-100$ death penalty), deterministic-preferred labels dominate (86:9). In MATH (no death penalty, $N{=}795$), labels are balanced (95:107), with 74.6\% no preference.}
62	\label{fig:signal-reversal}
63	\end{figure}
64	
65	\paragraph{Summary.} Table~\ref{tab:cross-env} and Figure~\ref{fig:signal-reversal} summarize the three environments. The contrast is systematic: ScienceWorld's death penalty produces strongly asymmetric labels (86:9); MATH's absence of catastrophic failure produces balanced labels (95:107); ALFWorld's information gap produces moderately asymmetric labels (35:2) through a distinct mechanism. These results establish boundary conditions for the death-avoidance finding rather than claiming its universality.
66	
67	\subsection{Baselines}
68	\label{sec:baselines}
69	
70	\begin{table}[t]
71	\centering
72	\small
73	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
74	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
75	\toprule
76	Strategy & Score & 95\% CI & Death \% \\
77	\midrule
78	Always-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\
79	Always-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\
80	Random & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\
81	Oracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\
82	\midrule
83	Trained Router$^\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\
84	\bottomrule
85	\end{tabular}}
86	\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 tasks, 245 episodes). $\dagger$\,Single seed; see \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}.}
87	\label{tab:baselines}
88	\end{table}
89	
90	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic, an \emph{oracle degeneracy} indicating the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.
91	
92	\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}
93	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
94	
95	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA fine-tuned; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) is trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with identical 4-seed configurations. Step-level balanced accuracy is flat across scales ($56.4 \pm 3.5\%$, $58.2 \pm 4.0\%$, $59.2 \pm 3.5\%$; all within seed variance); step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}), while 0.6B ($50.7 \pm 2.6\%$) and 1.7B ($43.1 \pm 1.0\%$) remain at or below chance. The 1.7B dip is robust across seeds (range $41.7$--$44.4\%$); this non-monotonic pattern, combined with universally low internal recall, reinforces that learned routing does not scale predictably with capacity at current data scales.
96	
97	\paragraph{Data scaling.} Augmentation-based data scaling ($25\%$--$800\%$) provides no improvement (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}), but paraphrase augmentation inflates the majority class (89.6\%$\rightarrow$97.7\%) without generating novel routing patterns; real-data scaling (requiring ${\sim}1{,}350$ episode pairs for 50 minority-class examples) remains an open question. Capacity scaling improves episode-level routing ($+9$\,pp at 4B) without improving step-level accuracy (flat ${\sim}57\%$); the extractable signal is task-type-level structure.

Score distributions are comparable across the shared task types.} to test whether improved episode-level accuracy translates to deployment gains. The 4B router scores $-10.72$ (95\% CI: $[-15.69, -5.52]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). The 1.7B router ($-10.70$; Table~\ref{tab:baselines}) performs comparably. Despite improved episode-level accuracy ($+9$\,pp at 4B), deployment score remains flat at ${\sim}{-10.7}$: the DAF decomposition identifies this ceiling without requiring expensive router training at multiple scales. Label quality is verified in Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}.

101	Failure mode and ablation details in Appendices~\ref{app:failure-modes}--\ref{app:ablations}.

The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction is 78.9\\% (75 of 95 non-tie labels). Of these 75, 54 (72.0\\%) reduce to ties when the death penalty is removed entirely: in those episodes, both modalities score zero without death, so the only reason one strategy outscored the other was avoiding the $-100$ penalty. Death avoidance is not merely the dominant but often the \\emph{sole} discriminating signal between modalities. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score, but the death penalty still accounts for the majority of the score gap.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}\nThe internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = P \\cdot \\delta + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ makes $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\\delta$ with residual $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \\rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \\leq 25$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 9 \\texttt{internal\\_preferred}, and 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference}.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal concentration.}\nThe Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\\%$ of total death-rate differential. One outlier, \\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, has zero deaths ($\\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores alone); all 10 remaining types exhibit death-asymmetry-driven routing labels. Death avoidance is systematic across all task types exhibiting routing signal (11/30 after prescreening), not driven by a few outlier tasks.\n\n\\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.}\nEpisode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$) yields a 95\\% CI of $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for the aggregate DAF, excluding both 50\\% and 100\\%. This CI quantifies within-sample uncertainty; generalization to unseen task types requires held-out evaluation. Per-task CIs confirm $\\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types; \\texttt{chemistry-mix} ($N{=}20$, CI $[-5.0, 48.6]$) is the sole exception, attributable to small sample size. As a sensitivity analysis accounting for within-task-type correlation, cluster bootstrap resampling ($K{=}11$ task-type clusters, $B = 10{,}000$) yields a wider 95\\% CI $[73.3\\%, 100\\%]$. With $K{=}11$ clusters, this interval should be interpreted cautiously: \\citet{cameron-2008} recommend $K \\geq 40$ for reliable cluster bootstrap inference. The lower bound nonetheless remains well above 50\\%.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nA Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\\citep{llama3-2024} replication on 7 task types ($N{=}70$) shows the death-avoidance pattern reproduces and intensifies: internal death rate $34.3\\%$ vs.\\ deterministic $0\\%$ (cf.\\ Qwen: $42.9\\%$ vs.\\ $16.3\\%$), with zero internal-preferred episodes (24 det-preferred, 46 no-preference). With $N{=}70$ and zero internal-preferred labels, this preliminary pilot demonstrates directional consistency of the death mechanism across model families, not full framework replication, which would require a larger sample and independent environment. Both models are in the 7--8B range; 70B-class models and Reflexion-augmented reasoning represent testable predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Domain Negative Contrast: MATH}\n\\label{sec:math-contrast}\n\nOn MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy: $73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$ ($+0.5$~pp, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp). The McNemar test compares binary correctness: 80 problems are solved only by internal reasoning and 76 only by code, yielding near-symmetric discordance ($\\chi^2 = 0.10$). The label distribution is balanced: 107 \\texttt{int\\_preferred} vs.\\ 95 \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, with 74.6\\% \\texttt{no\\_preference}. The confirmed null rules out any routing signal of magnitude ${\\geq}3.6$~pp at $\\alpha{=}0.05$. The absence of systematic routing signal confirms that without catastrophic failure, modality routing confers no advantage in this domain.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment Contrasts}\n\\label{sec:cross-env}\n\nThe framework generates testable predictions beyond ScienceWorld. We evaluate two environments that lack catastrophic death penalties, testing whether the death-avoidance mechanism is specific to environments with binary catastrophic failure.\n\n\\paragraph{ALFWorld.} We apply the framework to ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021}, a text-based household environment (6 task types, 135 games, max 50 steps, binary success metric). Unlike ScienceWorld, ALFWorld has no death penalty; failure means exhausting the step budget. The two modalities differ in \\emph{information access}: the internal strategy receives only free-text observations, while the deterministic strategy additionally receives admissible actions at each step (an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch). The success rate gap is large: 27.4\\% (deterministic) vs.\\ 3.0\\% (internal), reflecting the 7B model's near-inability to solve household tasks without admissible-action guidance. The label distribution shows 35 deterministic-preferred (25.9\\%), 2 internal-preferred (1.5\\%), and 98 no-preference (72.6\\%), of which 96 are both-fail. The deterministic advantage stems entirely from the information gap rather than death avoidance, a qualitatively different routing mechanism that the framework diagnoses without modification.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nEnvironment & $N$ & Det\\% & Int\\% & NoPref\\% & Death? \\\\\n\\midrule\nScienceWorld & 245 & 35.1 & 3.7 & 61.2 & Yes ($-100$) \\\\\nMATH & 795 & 12.0 & 13.5 & 74.6 & No \\\\\nALFWorld & 135 & 25.9 & 1.5 & 72.6 & No\\textsuperscript{$\\dagger$} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Cross-environment label distribution. ```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	A Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.
7	Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.
8	More recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \cite{mtrouter-2026}.
9	The shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.
10	But this assumption has never been systematically verified: \emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}
11	If routing value traces to a single failure mode, practitioners can replace complex learned routers with simpler safety-aware policies at lower computational cost and deployment risk.
12	
13	We propose a \emph{formal diagnostic framework} for answering this question. The framework comprises (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), a penalty-dependent metric that decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding that holds regardless of how death is penalized. Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$), consistent with the predicted value from observed $\delta{=}0.265$ and $P{=}100$.
14	The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.
15	Once death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}).
16	
17	This framework addresses a methodological gap.
18	The standard approach to generating routing labels, per-step oracle comparison, degenerates under sparse rewards.
19	In ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, so no step-level signal exists (\S\ref{sec:collection}).
20	Keyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\S\ref{sec:baselines}).
21	Our counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\S\ref{sec:collection}). The framework is applicable to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes, requiring only paired episode outcomes under two modalities.
22	
23	Two cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.
24	First, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$).
25	Without death risk, modality routing confers no systematic advantage.
26	Second, a cross-model replication with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, 70 episode pairs) shows direction-consistent death patterns for all task types, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}); the phenomenon generalizes across model families.
27	An exploratory router (Qwen3 with LoRA, 0.6B--4B) trained on these counterfactual labels yields flat step-level balanced accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0\%$), while smaller scales remain at or below chance. Data scaling ($32{\times}$) ablations confirm this is structural rather than data-limited (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
28	
29	Our contributions are:
30	\begin{enumerate}
31	    \item \textbf{A formal episode-level counterfactual framework (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}) with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics} for decomposing routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. The framework applies to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:daf}).
32	
33	    \item \textbf{Empirical application to ScienceWorld} revealing that 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels are driven by death asymmetry (penalty-independent), with DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ (penalty-dependent). Router training yields flat step-level accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the baseline ($59.0\%$), and the cost is disproportionate to routing benefit. Death dominance renders always-deterministic near-optimal (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
34	
35	    \item \textbf{Cross-environment diagnostic contrasts} establishing framework boundary conditions: MATH ($N{=}795$) confirms the predicted null ($p = 0.81$, no routing signal without catastrophic failure); ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) identifies an alternative mechanism (information asymmetry); a Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct pilot confirms directional consistency across model families. True quantitative DAF replication requires a second catastrophic-failure environment (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
36	\end{enumerate}
37	
38	
39	
```
