Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$), consistent with the predicted value from observed $\\delta{=}0.265$ and $P{=}100$.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nThis framework addresses a methodological gap.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels, per-step oracle comparison, degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, so no step-level signal exists (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nOur counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}). The framework is applicable to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes, requiring only paired episode outcomes under two modalities.\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$).\nWithout death risk, modality routing confers no systematic advantage.\nSecond, a cross-model replication with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, 70 episode pairs) shows direction-consistent death patterns for all task types, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}); the phenomenon generalizes across model families.\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 with LoRA, 0.6B--4B) trained on these counterfactual labels yields flat step-level balanced accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0\\%$), while smaller scales remain at or below chance. Data scaling ($32{\\times}$) ablations confirm this is structural rather than data-limited (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{A formal episode-level counterfactual framework (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}) with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics} for decomposing routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. The framework applies to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical application to ScienceWorld} revealing that 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels are driven by death asymmetry (penalty-independent), with DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (penalty-dependent). Router training yields flat step-level accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the baseline ($59.0\\%$), and the cost is disproportionate to routing benefit. Death dominance renders always-deterministic near-optimal (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-environment diagnostic contrasts} establishing framework boundary conditions: MATH ($N{=}795$) confirms the predicted null ($p = 0.81$, no routing signal without catastrophic failure); ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) identifies an alternative mechanism (information asymmetry); a Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct pilot confirms directional consistency across model families. True quantitative DAF replication requires a second catastrophic-failure environment (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\n",
      "numLines": 39,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 39
    }
  },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01P3UGKaV2Wqpfba7HEKaN11`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Cross-environment evidence.}
5	The framework generates testable predictions beyond ScienceWorld. The MATH null ($p = 0.81$) validates the prediction that removing catastrophic failure eliminates routing signal; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism (\S\ref{sec:cross-env}). True quantitative DAF replication requires a second catastrophic-failure environment (e.g., NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020}, Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Penalty sensitivity and correlation structure.}
8	The $r = 0.97$ correlation is specific to catastrophic death ($P{=}100$); defining failure as any non-positive score yields $r = -0.13$ ($p = 0.70$). As shown in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}, this correlation follows algebraically from death dominance when $P$ is constant; larger models with lower death rates would reduce DAF. This prediction assumes models above a minimum capability threshold; preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests very weak models may exhibit low death rates not from avoidance ability but from inability to reach dangerous states.
9	
10	\paragraph{Implications for router design.}
11	Death avoidance dominance (78.9\% of non-tie labels, DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$) reframes modality routing as a \emph{safety} problem, closer to a conservative default policy than to cost-quality optimization~\citep{garcia2015comprehensive, cogrouter-2026}. The underlying failure mechanism is state hallucination: the internal strategy predicts that an action succeeded when the environment state did not change, and these prediction errors compound across steps until they trigger death. The deterministic strategy eliminates these compounding observation errors, while action-selection errors (16.3\% deterministic death rate) remain comparatively rare. The 86:9 label imbalance explains why always-deterministic is a strong baseline; 4B parameters for $+9$\,pp router improvement is consistent with the analytical framework being more efficient. This dominance is so thorough that even an oracle routing strategy with perfect per-task death risk knowledge cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}, Table~\ref{tab:threshold-routing}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Framework applicability.}
14	The framework applies most directly to environments with sparse terminal rewards and catastrophic failure modes. Environments with graded failure costs would yield intermediate DAF values; stronger backbones with lower hallucination rates would reduce death-rate differentials and consequently lower DAF.
15	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut this assumption has never been systematically verified: \\emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}\nIf routing value traces to a single failure mode, practitioners can replace complex learned routers with simpler safety-aware policies at lower computational cost and deployment risk.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{formal diagnostic framework} for answering this question. The framework comprises (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), a penalty-dependent metric that decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding that holds regardless of how death is penalized. Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$), consistent with the predicted value from observed $\\delta{=}0.265$ and $P{=}100$.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nThis framework addresses a methodological gap.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels, per-step oracle comparison, degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, so no step-level signal exists (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nOur counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}). The framework is applicable to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes, requiring only paired episode outcomes under two modalities.\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$).\nWithout death risk, modality routing confers no systematic advantage.\nSecond, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, $N{=}70$) shows direction-consistent death patterns, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 with LoRA, 0.6B--4B) trained on these counterfactual labels yields flat step-level balanced accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0\\%$), while smaller scales remain at or below chance. Data scaling ($32{\\times}$) ablations confirm this is structural rather than data-limited (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{A formal episode-level counterfactual framework (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}) with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics} for decomposing routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. The framework applies to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical application to ScienceWorld} revealing that 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels are driven by death asymmetry (penalty-independent), with DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (penalty-dependent). Router training yields flat step-level accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the baseline ($59.0\\%$), and the cost is disproportionate to routing benefit. Death dominance renders always-deterministic near-optimal (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Preliminary cross-environment diagnostic contrasts}: MATH ($N{=}795$) confirms the predicted null ($p = 0.81$, no routing signal without catastrophic failure); ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) provides an illustrative contrast where the framework identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism under fundamentally different modality definitions. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct pilot ($N{=}70$) provides preliminary evidence of cross-model directional consistency (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-env}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\n",

    "newString": "Second, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, $N{=}70$) shows direction-consistent death patterns, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nA Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.\nToolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \\cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.\nMore recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \\cite{mtrouter-2026}.\nThe shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.\nBut this assumption has never been systematically verified: \\emph{what actually drives the value of routing decisions in interactive environments?}\nIf routing value traces to a single failure mode, practitioners can replace complex learned routers with simpler safety-aware policies at lower computational cost and deployment risk.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{formal diagnostic framework} for answering this question. The framework comprises (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), a penalty-dependent metric that decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding that holds regardless of how death is penalized. Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$), consistent with the predicted value from observed $\\delta{=}0.265$ and $P{=}100$.\nThe internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.\nOnce death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n\nThis framework addresses a methodological gap.\nThe standard approach to generating routing labels, per-step oracle comparison, degenerates under sparse rewards.\nIn ScienceWorld, non-zero reward arrives only at episode termination; 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical scores regardless of modality, so no step-level signal exists (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}).\nKeyword-based heuristics also fail structurally, as ScienceWorld actions lack science-specific terminology (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}).\nOur counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes to generate routing labels (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}). The framework is applicable to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes, requiring only paired episode outcomes under two modalities.\n\nTwo cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.\nFirst, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems), using a code interpreter as the deterministic modality, confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$).\nWithout death risk, modality routing confers no systematic advantage.\nSecond, a cross-model replication with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, 70 episode pairs) shows direction-consistent death patterns for all task types, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}); the phenomenon generalizes across model families.\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 with LoRA, 0.6B--4B) trained on these counterfactual labels yields flat step-level balanced accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0\\%$), while smaller scales remain at or below chance. Data scaling ($32{\\times}$) ablations confirm this is structural rather than data-limited (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{A formal episode-level counterfactual framework (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}) with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics} for decomposing routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. The framework applies to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical application to ScienceWorld} revealing that 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels are driven by death asymmetry (penalty-independent), with DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (penalty-dependent). Router training yields flat step-level accuracy across scales ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the baseline ($59.0\\%$), and the cost is disproportionate to routing benefit. Death dominance renders always-deterministic near-optimal (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Preliminary cross-environment diagnostic contrasts}: MATH ($N{=}795$) confirms the predicted null ($p = 0.81$, no routing signal without catastrophic failure); ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) provides an illustrative contrast where the framework identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism under fundamentally different modality definitions. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct pilot ($N{=}70$) provides preliminary evidence of cross-model directional consistency (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-env}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe presented a formal diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents via episode-level counterfactual analysis with dual metrics (penalty-independent label fraction and penalty-dependent DAF). Applied to 245 ScienceWorld episodes (11 of 30 task types, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), death asymmetry accounts for 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels; DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$). Cross-domain contrasts confirm framework predictions: MATH shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure; ALFWorld reveals information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism; a Llama pilot confirms cross-model consistency. Open directions include replication in catastrophic-failure environments (e.g., NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020}, Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}) and stronger backbones, which the framework predicts will lower DAF.\n",

An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA fine-tuned; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) is trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with identical 4-seed configurations. Step-level balanced accuracy is flat across scales ($56.4 \\pm 3.5\\%$, $58.2 \\pm 4.0\\%$, $59.2 \\pm 3.5\\%$; all within seed variance); step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}), while 0.6B ($50.7 \\pm 2.6\\%$) and 1.7B ($43.1 \\pm 1.0\\%$) remain at or below chance.           " \\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} We deploy the best router (4B) in the full ScienceWorld loop ($N{=}275$ episodes)\\footnote{The 4B evaluation uses $N{=}275$ episodes (vs.\\ $N{=}245$ for baselines in Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}) due to expanded task coverage during data collection. 18	\title{Death Avoidance as a Dominant Routing Signal:\\A Counterfactual Analysis on ScienceWorld}
19	
20	\author{Anonymous}
21	
22	\begin{document}
23	\maketitle
24	
25	\input{abstract}

32	
33	\section*{Limitations}
34	
35	Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface additional signal. Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\citep{react-2023}; more sophisticated strategies (e.g., Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\citep{tot-2023}) may reduce internal death rates and lower DAF. Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing to individual steps, particularly those preceding death events, and training on pure-strategy trajectories introduces distribution shift at deployment~\citep{dagger-2011}. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\S\ref{sec:cross-env}, \S\ref{sec:discussion}) provide supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; validation at larger scales remains future work.
36	
37	\section*{Ethics Statement}
38	
39	This work uses ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. We report computational costs: approximately 40 GPU-hours on two consumer GPUs for data collection, router training, and evaluation. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
40	
41	\bibliography{references}
42	
43	\appendix
44	
45	\section{Signal Concentration Index}
46	\label{app:sci}
47	
48	Let $K$ denote the number of task types and $c_k$ the contribution of task type $k$ to the aggregate routing signal, defined as the fraction of total death-rate differential attributable to task $k$:
49	\begin{equation}
50	    c_k = \frac{n_k \cdot \delta_k}{\sum_{j=1}^{K} n_j \cdot \delta_j}
51	\end{equation}
52	where $n_k$ is the number of episodes and $\delta_k = d_k^{\tt int} - d_k^{\tt det}$ is the per-task death-rate differential. The SCI is the Gini coefficient over $\{c_1, \ldots, c_K\}$:
53	\begin{equation}
54	    \text{SCI} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^K \sum_{j=1}^K |c_i - c_j|}{2K \sum_{k=1}^K c_k}
55	\end{equation}
56	
57	\section{Labeling Quality Audit}
58	\label{app:labeling}
59	
60	A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \texttt{no\_preference} labels (61.2\%), 115 (46.9\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\%) are both-dead, all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\%, within 1$\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\pm$ 0.5\%, step-level Trainer evaluation); score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 97.4\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.
61	
62	\section{Per-Task Decomposition}
63	\label{app:per-task}
64	We select 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types via prescreening: task types where both strategies complete at least one episode within the 30-step budget are retained; types where both strategies time out on all seed variations are excluded.\footnote{Prescreening ensures that the counterfactual comparison is meaningful: task types where neither strategy makes progress provide no routing signal.}

87	\textbf{All (11 tasks)} & \textbf{245} & $\mathbf{+29.60}$ & \textbf{42.9} & \textbf{16.3} & \textbf{86 / 9 / 150} \\
88	\bottomrule
89	\end{tabular}
90	\caption{Per-task decomposition of the death avoidance effect. D\%: death rate for internal (int) and deterministic (det) strategies. D/I/N: episode labels (det-preferred / internal-preferred / no-preference). Sorted by $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ descending. $\dagger$\,Only task with zero deaths under both strategies; $n{=}40$ because data was collected in two batches, with the second batch adding 20 episodes to increase statistical power for this zero-death outlier.}
91	\label{tab:per-task}
92	\end{table}
93	
94	\paragraph{Task selection and anti-cherry-picking.}
95	The 11 included task types are those exhibiting meaningful routing signal in prescreening, not those with the highest death rates. The deterministic-strategy death rate is comparable between included (17.8\%) and excluded (14.9\%) task types; prescreening does not select for death-prone tasks. Rather, the key distinction is the \emph{presence} of routing signal: all 11 included tasks have $\Delta_{\text{nd}} \neq 0$ (non-zero performance gap on surviving episodes), while 53\% of excluded tasks show $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 0$ (neither strategy outperforms the other even when both survive, typically because both time out before making progress). \texttt{find-non-living-thing} serves as an internal counterexample: it has zero deaths under both strategies yet is included because it exhibits non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ entirely from score differences), and deterministic is not always preferred (4 of 20 non-tie labels favor internal).
96

Spearman rank correlation is $\rho = 0.986$ ($p = 2.4 \times 10^{-8}$); the relationship is not driven by outliers or non-linearity. A nonparametric bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples) yields 95\% CI $[0.952, 0.995]$.

125	
126	\section{Router Training Details}
127	\label{app:router-details}
128	
129	We use Qwen3-1.7B for the router to avoid circular dependency with the Qwen2.5-7B data-generating model.
130	
131	With gradient accumulation of 1 (effective batch size 4), routing performance is highly sensitive to the evaluation split; this suggests overfitting to specific train/validation partition boundaries. With gradient accumulation of 8 (effective batch size 32), step-level performance stabilizes across splits (${\sim}54\%$ balanced accuracy). All reported results use gradient accumulation 8.
132	
133	Under step-level evaluation with a fixed data split, cross-seed balanced accuracy ranges from 52.7\% to 58.1\%, but internal recall varies from 51.5\% to 81.8\%, reflecting threshold sensitivity. However, episode-level evaluation reveals that this apparent discrimination does not generalize: balanced accuracy drops to $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ across 8 cross-split seeds (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}). Deployed end-to-end, the trained router allocates 97.0\% of episodes to deterministic computation with 17.6\% death rate, and misrouting cost (death from internally-routed episodes) exceeds the benefit of correctly routing rare internal-preferred cases.
134	
135	\paragraph{Evaluation methods.} All results use \emph{generation-based evaluation}: the model generates a routing label as text (\texttt{enable\_thinking=False}), parsed for classification. We evaluate at two granularities: \emph{step-level} (steps from the same episode may appear in both train and validation sets) and \emph{episode-level} (entire episodes held out). Step-level accuracy ($54.1 \pm 5.4\%$) exceeds chance, indicating the router captures some signal, but episode-level accuracy ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$, below chance) shows this does not generalize to unseen episodes. Augmentation-based data scaling (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}) provides no improvement; real-data scaling remains open. Always-deterministic is trivially near-optimal given DAF $= 90.4\%$. The main text (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reports controlled 4-seed results at three scales; the model-size ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}) provides further detail.
136	
137	\begin{table}[h]
138	\centering
139	\small
140	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
141	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
142	\toprule
143	Hyperparameter & Classification Head & Implicit Router \\
144	\midrule
145	Base model & Qwen3-1.7B & Qwen3-1.7B \\
146	Architecture & Linear + LoRA & SFT + LoRA \\
147	LoRA $r$ / $\alpha$ & 16 / 32 & 16 / 32 \\
148	Learning rate & 2e-5 & 2e-4 \\
149	Optimizer & AdamW & AdamW \\
150	Epochs & 3 & 3 \\
151	Batch size (per device) & 4 & 4 \\
152	Gradient accumulation & 8 & 8 \\
153	Warmup ratio & 0.1 & 0.1 \\
154	Weight decay & 0.01 & 0.01 \\
155	Max sequence length & 512 & 512 \\
156	Seeds & \{42\} & \{42, 123, 456, 789\} \\
157	\bottomrule
158	\end{tabular}}
159	\caption{Router training hyperparameters. Both architectures use the same base model with LoRA adaptation. The classification head degenerates across all configurations; the implicit router results are reported as 4-seed mean$\pm$std.}
160	\label{tab:hyperparams}
161	\end{table}
162	
163	\section{Seed Robustness}
164	\label{app:seed-robustness}
165	
166	We assess router generalization at two evaluation granularities: \emph{step-level}, where steps from the same episode may appear in both train and validation sets, and \emph{episode-level}, where entire episodes are held out. Both use cross-split evaluation (seeds $\{42, \ldots, 49\}$, 8 seeds total), where the data partition and training seed both vary. Table~\ref{tab:seed-robustness} reports per-seed results.
167	
168	\begin{table}[h]
169	\centering
170	\small
171	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}
172	\toprule
173	Eval.\ Granularity & Seed & Bal.\ Acc.\ (\%) \\
174	\midrule
175	Step-level & 42 & 57.06 \\
176	(same-episode & 43 & 63.99 \\
177	leakage) & 44 & 55.43 \\
178	 & 45 & 56.14 \\
179	 & 46 & 55.45 \\
180	 & 47 & 50.08 \\
181	 & 48 & 48.03 \\
182	 & 49 & 59.30 \\
183	\cmidrule{2-3}
184	 & Mean$\pm$std & $54.1 \pm 5.4$ \\
185	\midrule
186	Episode-level & 42 & 48.1 \\
187	(held-out & 43 & 47.9 \\
188	episodes) & 44 & 49.6 \\
189	 & 45 & 49.4 \\
190	 & 46 & 48.5 \\
191	 & 47 & 43.1 \\
192	 & 48 & 45.4 \\
193	 & 49 & 44.8 \\
194	\cmidrule{2-3}
195	 & Mean$\pm$std & $47.1 \pm 2.2$ \\
196	\midrule
197	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{Always-det (= majority-vote)} & 50.0 \\
198	\bottomrule
199	\end{tabular}
200	\caption{Per-seed router performance under cross-split evaluation (8 seeds, generation-based, \texttt{enable\_thinking=False}). Step-level evaluation allows steps from the same episode in both train and validation sets (same-episode leakage). Episode-level evaluation holds out entire episodes; the resulting $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ is below the 50\% chance baseline. A data scaling ablation (Table~\ref{tab:data-scaling}) confirms this is structural rather than data-limited.}
201	\label{tab:seed-robustness}
202	\end{table}
203	
204	The contrast between step-level ($54.1 \pm 5.4\%$) and episode-level ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$) evaluation reveals a generalization gap: the step-level signal likely reflects memorization of episode-specific context (enabled by same-episode leakage) rather than extraction of generalizable routing features. At episode level, the router performs below chance across all 8 seeds (range: 43.1\%--49.6\%). Augmentation-based data scaling (Table~\ref{tab:data-scaling}) from 25\% to 800\% ($32{\times}$ range) provides no improvement, converging to the always-deterministic baseline ($50\%$). However, paraphrase augmentation inflates the majority class without generating novel routing patterns; real-data scaling remains an open question. With DAF $= 90.4\%$ and all task types deterministic-majority (86:9 label ratio), always-deterministic trivially solves the episode-level routing problem, leaving no learnable signal for a step-level router.
205	
206	\paragraph{Data scaling ablation.} To test whether the episode-level generalization failure stems from data scarcity, we vary training set size from 25\% to 800\% of the original via paraphrase-based data augmentation (synonym substitution + template variation on step observations), holding the episode-level validation set fixed. Table~\ref{tab:data-scaling} reports the results.
207	
208	\begin{table}[h]
209	\centering
210	\small
211	\begin{tabular}{@{}rccc@{}}
212	\toprule
213	Scale & Samples & Det\% & Ep.\ Bal.\ Acc.\ (\%) \\
214	\midrule
215	25\% & 433 & ${\sim}90$ & $49.5 \pm 0.3$ \\
216	50\% & 867 & ${\sim}90$ & $51.5$ \\
217	100\% & 1{,}735 & 89.6 & $49.9 \pm 0.1$ \\
218	200\% & 3{,}470 & 94.2 & $50.0 \pm 0.0$ \\
219	800\% & 13{,}880 & ${\sim}97.7$ & $51.0 \pm 1.1$ \\
220	\midrule
221	Always-det & --- & --- & 50.0 \\
222	\bottomrule
223	\end{tabular}
224	\caption{Data scaling ablation: episode-level balanced accuracy across training set sizes (25\%--800\% of original, $32{\times}$ range). Internal recall $= 0.0$ at all scales (complete majority-class collapse). Det\%: fraction of deterministic-preferred labels in training data. The 50\% scale is a single seed; all others report mean$\pm$std across seeds.}
225	\label{tab:data-scaling}
226	\end{table}
227	
228	All scales hover at ${\sim}50\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy (equivalent to the always-deterministic baseline), with no monotonic improvement as data increases. Augmentation-based scaling provides no improvement at any scale; however, episode-level minority-class diversity remains a potential confound that cannot be resolved without collecting more internal-preferred episodes. The ground-truth internal-preferred rate (9/245 $= 3.7\%$) means collecting 50 internal-preferred episodes would require approximately 1{,}350 paired episodes at current environment dynamics, a $5.5{\times}$ expansion with diminishing return on a $9.6\%$ signal. The failure points instead to a structural limitation: when DAF $= 90.4\%$ and all task types are deterministic-majority (86:9 label ratio), the episode-level routing problem is trivially solved by always choosing deterministic, leaving no learnable signal for a step-level router to exploit.
229	
230	Augmentation disproportionately increases the majority (deterministic) class: Det\% rises from 89.6\% at 100\% to 94.2\% at 200\% and ${\sim}97.7\%$ at 800\%. However, the complete collapse to majority-class prediction (internal recall $= 0.0$) occurs even at the smallest unaugmented scale (25\%, ${\sim}90\%$ det); this is not an augmentation artifact. Balanced accuracy corrects for class imbalance by averaging per-class recall, so the metric itself is not biased by the skew.
231	
232	Decomposition metrics are stable under bootstrap resampling (1{,}000 resamples of the 245-episode dataset): DAF $= 90.6\% \pm 4.2\%$ (95\% CI $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$); Pearson $r = 0.975 \pm 0.011$ (95\% CI $[0.952, 0.995]$). The death avoidance dominance finding is robust to both resampling and split perturbation.
233	
234	\paragraph{Per-task-type evaluation breakdown.} To test whether the router learns transferable routing strategies or merely task-type classification, we evaluate existing checkpoints (8 seeds) separately on each of the 11 task types. Of the 11 task types, 7 have \emph{all-deterministic} labels (no internal-preferred episodes exist), making balanced accuracy undefined; the router has nothing to learn beyond the trivial always-deterministic default. For the remaining 4 task types where both classes are present, balanced accuracy ranges from 50.0\% to 73.0\%, but this apparent signal does not indicate cross-task-type routing transfer: the router is effectively classifying task types (which correlate with death risk) rather than extracting a generalizable routing strategy. This complements the data scaling ablation: routing signal does not transfer across task types because the signal \emph{is} the task type, i.e., whether a given task carries death risk. The 4B capacity scaling result (Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}) suggests capacity is one factor, but even 4B's $59.0\%$ episode-level accuracy does not demonstrate cross-task-type transfer.
235	
236	\section{Penalty Sensitivity Analysis}
237	\label{app:penalty}
238	
239	\begin{table}[h]
240	\centering
241	\small
242	\begin{tabular}{@{}rrr@{}}
243	\toprule
244	$P$ & DAF($P$) & 95\% CI \\
245	\midrule
246	0 & 7.5\% & [$-36.7$, $83.1$] \\
247	10 & 50.4\% & [$19.8$, $94.5$] \\
248	25 & 70.8\% & [$48.5$, $97.1$] \\
249	50 & 82.6\% & [$67.6$, $98.4$] \\
250	100\textsuperscript{*} & 90.4\% & [$81.5$, $99.1$] \\
251	200 & 95.0\% & [$90.0$, $99.5$] \\
252	500 & 97.9\% & [$95.8$, $99.8$] \\
253	\bottomrule
254	\end{tabular}
255	\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. DAF$(P) = 1 - \Delta_{\text{nd}} / \Delta_{\text{full}}(P)$, where $\Delta_{\text{full}}(P) \approx \delta \cdot P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$, $\delta = 0.2653$ is the death-rate differential, and $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (strict neither-died filtering). Bootstrap 95\% CIs from 1{,}000 resamples. \textsuperscript{*}Default setting.}
256	\label{tab:penalty-sensitivity}
257	\end{table}
258	
259	The non-death score gap $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant across penalty levels; it depends only on surviving-episode performance, not on $P$. As $P$ increases, $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ grows ($16.33$ at $P{=}50$, $29.60$ at $P{=}100$, $56.13$ at $P{=}200$) while $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ remains fixed, mechanically increasing DAF.
260	
261	\paragraph{Mechanism boundary: death specificity.}
262	The $r = 0.97$ correlation between per-task death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ is specific to catastrophic death (score $= -100$). Broader failure definitions dissolve the correlation entirely: defining ``failure'' as any non-positive score ($T{=}0$) yields $r = -0.13$ ($p = 0.70$), because non-death episodes show near-zero performance differential between strategies ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$). Table~\ref{tab:penalty-correlation} quantifies this specificity across penalty magnitudes.
263	
264	\begin{table}[h]
265	\centering
266	\small
267	\begin{tabular}{@{}rccl@{}}
268	\toprule
269	$P$ & $r$ & $p$ & Sig.\ ($\alpha{=}0.05$) \\
270	\midrule
271	10 & $-0.08$ & $0.813$ & No \\
272	25 & $0.51$ & $0.108$ & No \\
273	50 & $0.86$ & $0.0006$ & Yes \\
274	100\textsuperscript{*} & $0.97$ & $8 \times 10^{-7}$ & Yes \\
275	200 & $0.99$ & $1 \times 10^{-9}$ & Yes \\
276	\bottomrule
277	\end{tabular}
278	\caption{Pearson $r$ between per-task death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{full}}(P)$ across penalty magnitudes. The correlation is non-significant at low penalties and emerges at $P \geq 50$, reflecting the transition from non-death-dominated to death-dominated score gaps. \textsuperscript{*}Default ScienceWorld setting.}
279	\label{tab:penalty-correlation}
280	\end{table}
281	
282	This sharpens our central finding: death is not merely the dominant component of routing signal; it is effectively the \emph{only} component that generates differential routing signal in ScienceWorld. At low penalties, the death contribution is small and $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ reduces to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} \approx 2.84$, which is near-uniform across task types ($\text{mean non-death gap} = -1.19$ excluding the \texttt{find-non-living-thing} outlier); consequently, death-rate differential has no predictive power. As $P$ increases, the death term dominates $\Delta_{\text{full}}$, and the correlation sharpens because death rates \emph{are} the performance gap. The DAF values in Table~\ref{tab:penalty-sensitivity} increase monotonically with $P$, but this is a mathematical consequence of the decomposition ($\text{DAF} = \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}})$), not evidence of mechanism robustness.
283	
284	\section{Cross-Model Verification}
285	\label{app:cross-model}
286	
287	To test whether the death-avoidance finding generalizes beyond Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, we replicate the counterfactual analysis with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on 7 task types ($N{=}70$ episode pairs, 10 episodes per task). The results are strongly consistent with the Qwen findings: 24 episodes (34.3\%) are deterministic-preferred, \textbf{0} (0\%) are internal-preferred, and 46 (65.7\%) show no preference. The internal strategy dies in 58.6\% of episodes versus 24.3\% for deterministic (death-rate differential $+34.3\%$), compared to $+26.6\%$ for Qwen2.5-7B. Direction consistency is perfect: all 7 task types show higher internal than deterministic death rates (7/7, compared to 10/11 for Qwen). The larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.6\%$) and the absence of any internal-preferred episodes (0 vs.\ 9 for Qwen) suggest that Llama-3.1-8B is more vulnerable to hallucination-induced death, amplifying the death-avoidance effect. These results confirm that the death-avoidance dominance is not specific to the Qwen model family.
288	
289	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
290	\label{app:failure-modes}
291	
292	The most common failure mode for the internal strategy is state hallucination: the model predicts that an action succeeded (e.g., ``the water is now boiling'') when the environment state did not change, leading to cascading errors that trigger agent death. The deterministic strategy avoids this by receiving ground-truth observations, but still dies in 16.3\% of episodes, primarily from selecting inherently dangerous actions (e.g., eating toxic substances) rather than from state misestimation.
293	
294	\section{Ablation Studies}
295	\label{app:ablations}
296	
297	\paragraph{Capacity scaling.} We scale the implicit router from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B and evaluate at both step and episode level (Table~\ref{tab:capacity}).
298	
299	\begin{table}[h]
300	\centering
301	\small
302	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
303	\toprule
304	Model & Step Bal.\ Acc.\ (\%) & Ep.\ Bal.\ Acc.\ (\%) \\
305	\midrule
306	Qwen3-0.6B & $56.4 \pm 3.5$ & $50.7 \pm 2.6$ \\
307	Qwen3-1.7B & $58.2 \pm 4.0$ & $43.1 \pm 1.0$ \\
308	Qwen3-4B & $59.2 \pm 3.5$ & $\mathbf{59.0 \pm 1.9}$ \\
309	\midrule
310	Always-det & 50.0 & 50.0 \\

313	\caption{Capacity scaling: step-level and episode-level balanced accuracy (4 seeds, identical configuration across all scales). Step-level accuracy is flat ($56$--$59\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B breaks the random baseline ($59.0\%$, $p < 0.005$).}
314	\label{tab:capacity}
315	\end{table}
316	
317	Step-level balanced accuracy is flat across scales ($56$--$59\%$, within seed variance), indicating step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, only 4B breaks the random baseline ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.005$); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The dominant explanation is that death avoidance constitutes a task-type-level binary signal that a step-level router cannot exploit efficiently.
318	
319	\paragraph{Reasoning depth.} An ablation comparing chain-of-thought reasoning against direct-answer generation on 6 task types ($n{=}120$ episodes per condition, 6 tasks $\times$ 20 episodes per task) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, $|d| \leq 0.31$) and near-identical death rates (41.8\% vs.\ 42.5\%); reasoning depth does not meaningfully alter internal-mode outcomes.
320	
321	\section{Threshold Routing Analysis}
322	\label{sec:threshold-routing}
323	
324	Given per-task death risk knowledge, we test whether a simple threshold routing strategy can outperform always-deterministic. For each threshold $t \in [0, 1]$, we route to internal if the per-task internal death rate $\leq t$, otherwise to deterministic, and sweep all thresholds to find the optimal.
325

327	\centering

329	\begin{tabular}{lccc}

331	Strategy & Mean Score & Death Rate & $\Delta$ vs Det \\

335	Always-internal & $-39.07$ & $0.429$ & $-29.60$ \\
336	\bottomrule
337	\end{tabular}
338	\caption{Death-risk threshold routing analysis on 245 episodes. The optimal threshold routes only \texttt{find-non-living-thing} (the sole zero-death task) to internal, with all other tasks routed to deterministic. Bootstrap 95\% CI for the difference: $[-8.87, +5.92]$.}
339	\label{tab:threshold-routing}
340	\end{table}
341	
342	Even with perfect per-task death risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform always-deterministic. The sole zero-death task achieves higher scores under deterministic (+28.5) than internal (+18.9), leaving no room for a beneficial mixed strategy.

The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface additional signal. Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}; more sophisticated strategies (e.g., Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023}) may reduce internal death rates and lower DAF. Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing to individual steps, particularly those preceding death events, and training on pure-strategy trajectories introduces distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-env}, \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) provide supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; validation at larger scales remains future work.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. We report computational costs: approximately 40 GPU-hours on two consumer GPUs for data collection, router training, and evaluation. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\n\\section{Signal Concentration Index}\n\\label{app:sci}\n\nLet $K$ denote the number of task types and $c_k$ the contribution of task type $k$ to the aggregate routing signal, defined as the fraction of total death-rate differential attributable to task $k$:\n\\begin{equation}\n    c_k = \\frac{n_k \\cdot \\delta_k}{\\sum_{j=1}^{K} n_j \\cdot \\delta_j}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $n_k$ is the number of episodes and $\\delta_k = d_k^{\\tt int} - d_k^{\\tt det}$ is the per-task death-rate differential. The SCI is the Gini coefficient over $\\{c_1, \\ldots, c_K\\}$:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{SCI} = \\frac{\\sum_{i=1}^K \\sum_{j=1}^K |c_i - c_j|}{2K \\sum_{k=1}^K c_k}\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\section{Labeling Quality Audit}\n\\label{app:labeling}\n\nA potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference} labels (61.2\\%), 115 (46.9\\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\\%) are both-dead, all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. The 97.4\\% step-level tie rate (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.\n\n\\section{Per-Task Decomposition}\n\\label{app:per-task}\nWe select 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types via prescreening: task types where both strategies complete at least one episode within the 30-step budget are retained; types where both strategies time out on all seed variations are excluded.\\footnote{Prescreening ensures that the counterfactual comparison is meaningful: task types where neither strategy makes progress provide no routing signal.}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{2.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nTask & $n$ & $\\Delta_\\text{full}$ & D\\%\\textsubscript{int} & D\\%\\textsubscript{det} & D / I / N \\\\\n\\midrule\nid-life-stages-1       & 20  & $+47.50$ & 55   & 10   & 9 / 0 / 11  \\\\\nlifespan-long          & 25  & $+44.00$ & 56   & 16   & 11 / 1 / 13 \\\\\nlifespan-l-then-s      & 20  & $+40.80$ & 45   & 10   & 8 / 1 / 11  \\\\\ninclined-plane         & 20  & $+36.00$ & 55   & 20   & 7 / 0 / 13  \\\\\nfreeze                 & 20  & $+30.50$ & 55   & 25   & 6 / 0 / 14  \\\\\nmeas-melt-point        & 20  & $+30.05$ & 55   & 25   & 6 / 0 / 14  \\\\\nid-life-stages-2       & 20  & $+30.00$ & 50   & 20   & 6 / 0 / 14  \\\\\nfind-animal            & 20  & $+26.65$ & 50   & 25   & 5 / 0 / 15  \\\\\ngrow-fruit             & 20  & $+25.10$ & 45   & 20   & 6 / 1 / 13  \\\\\nchem-mix               & 20  & $+21.80$ & 45   & 25   & 6 / 2 / 12  \\\\\n\\midrule\nfind-non-living$^\\dagger$ & 40 & $+9.58$ & 0  & 0    & 16 / 4 / 20 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{All (11 tasks)} & \\textbf{245} & $\\mathbf{+29.60}$ & \\textbf{42.9} & \\textbf{16.3} & \\textbf{86 / 9 / 150} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Per-task decomposition of the death avoidance effect. All reported results use gradient accumulation 8.\n\nUnder step-level evaluation with a fixed data split, cross-seed balanced accuracy ranges from 52.7\\% to 58.1\\%, but internal recall varies from 51.5\\% to 81.8\\%, reflecting threshold sensitivity. Deployed end-to-end, the trained router allocates 97.0\\% of episodes to deterministic computation with 17.6\\% death rate, and misrouting cost (death from internally-routed episodes) exceeds the benefit of correctly routing rare internal-preferred cases.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation methods.} All results use \\emph{generation-based evaluation}: the model generates a routing label as text (\\texttt{enable\\_thinking=False}), parsed for classification. The main text (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reports controlled 4-seed results at three scales; the model-size ablation (Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}) provides further detail.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nHyperparameter & Classification Head & Implicit Router \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase model & Qwen3-1.7B & Qwen3-1.7B \\\\\nArchitecture & Linear + LoRA & SFT + LoRA \\\\\nLoRA $r$ / $\\alpha$ & 16 / 32 & 16 / 32 \\\\\nLearning rate & 2e-5 & 2e-4 \\\\\nOptimizer & AdamW & AdamW \\\\\nEpochs & 3 & 3 \\\\\nBatch size (per device) & 4 & 4 \\\\\nGradient accumulation & 8 & 8 \\\\\nWarmup ratio & 0.1 & 0.1 \\\\\nWeight decay & 0.01 & 0.01 \\\\\nMax sequence length & 512 & 512 \\\\\nSeeds & \\{42\\} & \\{42, 123, 456, 789\\} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Router training hyperparameters. Table~\\ref{tab:data-scaling} reports the results.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nScale & Samples & Det\\% & Ep.\\ Bal.\\ Acc.\\ (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\n25\\% & 433 & ${\\sim}90$ & $49.5 \\pm 0.3$ \\\\\n50\\% & 867 & ${\\sim}90$ & $51.5$ \\\\\n100\\% & 1{,}735 & 89.6 & $49.9 \\pm 0.1$ \\\\\n200\\% & 3{,}470 & 94.2 & $50.0 \\pm 0.0$ \\\\\n800\\% & 13{,}880 & ${\\sim}97.7$ & $51.0 \\pm 1.1$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-det & --- & --- & 50.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Data scaling ablation: episode-level balanced accuracy across training set sizes (25\\%--800\\% of original, $32{\\times}$ range). The 50\\% scale is a single seed; all others report mean$\\pm$std across seeds.}\n\\label{tab:data-scaling}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll scales hover at ${\\sim}50\\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy (equivalent to the always-deterministic baseline), with no monotonic improvement as data increases. The ground-truth internal-preferred rate (9/245 $= 3.7\\%$) means collecting 50 internal-preferred episodes would require approximately 1{,}350 paired episodes at current environment dynamics, a $5.5{\\times}$ expansion with diminishing return on a $9.6\\%$ signal. Balanced accuracy corrects for class imbalance by averaging per-class recall, so the metric itself is not biased by the skew.\n\nDecomposition metrics are stable under bootstrap resampling (1{,}000 resamples of the 245-episode dataset): DAF $= 90.6\\% \\pm 4.2\\%$ (95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$); Pearson $r = 0.975 \\pm 0.011$ (95\\% CI $[0.952, 0.995]$). The death avoidance dominance finding is robust to both resampling and split perturbation.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task-type evaluation breakdown.} To test whether the router learns transferable routing strategies or merely task-type classification, we evaluate existing checkpoints (8 seeds) separately on each of the 11 task types. For the remaining 4 task types where both classes are present, balanced accuracy ranges from 50.0\\% to 73.0\\%, but this apparent signal does not indicate cross-task-type routing transfer: the router is effectively classifying task types (which correlate with death risk) rather than extracting a generalizable routing strategy. This complements the data scaling ablation: routing signal does not transfer across task types because the signal \\emph{is} the task type, i.e., whether a given task carries death risk. The 4B capacity scaling result (Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}) suggests capacity is one factor, but even 4B's $59.0\\%$ episode-level accuracy does not demonstrate cross-task-type transfer.\n\n\\section{Penalty Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:penalty}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$P$ & DAF($P$) & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\n0 & 7.5\\% & [$-36.7$, $83.1$] \\\\\n10 & 50.4\\% & [$19.8$, $94.5$] \\\\\n25 & 70.8\\% & [$48.5$, $97.1$] \\\\\n50 & 82.6\\% & [$67.6$, $98.4$] \\\\\n100\\textsuperscript{*} & 90.4\\% & [$81.5$, $99.1$] \\\\\n200 & 95.0\\% & [$90.0$, $99.5$] \\\\\n500 & 97.9\\% & [$95.8$, $99.8$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs from 1{,}000 resamples. \\textsuperscript{*}Default setting.}\n\\label{tab:penalty-sensitivity}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe non-death score gap $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant across penalty levels; it depends only on surviving-episode performance, not on $P$. As $P$ increases, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ grows ($16.33$ at $P{=}50$, $29.60$ at $P{=}100$, $56.13$ at $P{=}200$) while $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ remains fixed, mechanically increasing DAF.\n\n\\paragraph{Mechanism boundary: death specificity.}\nThe $r = 0.97$ correlation between per-task death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ is specific to catastrophic death (score $= -100$). Broader failure definitions dissolve the correlation entirely: defining ``failure'' as any non-positive score ($T{=}0$) yields $r = -0.13$ ($p = 0.70$), because non-death episodes show near-zero performance differential between strategies ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$). Table~\\ref{tab:penalty-correlation} quantifies this specificity across penalty magnitudes.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rccl@{}}\n\\toprule\n$P$ & $r$ & $p$ & Sig.\\ ($\\alpha{=}0.05$) \\\\\n\\midrule\n10 & $-0.08$ & $0.813$ & No \\\\\n25 & $0.51$ & $0.108$ & No \\\\\n50 & $0.86$ & $0.0006$ & Yes \\\\\n100\\textsuperscript{*} & $0.97$ & $8 \\times 10^{-7}$ & Yes \\\\\n200 & $0.99$ & $1 \\times 10^{-9}$ & Yes \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Pearson $r$ between per-task death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}(P)$ across penalty magnitudes. At low penalties, the death contribution is small and $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ reduces to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} \\approx 2.84$, which is near-uniform across task types ($\\text{mean non-death gap} = -1.19$ excluding the \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} outlier); consequently, death-rate differential has no predictive power. As $P$ increases, the death term dominates $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$, and the correlation sharpens because death rates \\emph{are} the performance gap. The DAF values in Table~\\ref{tab:penalty-sensitivity} increase monotonically with $P$, but this is a mathematical consequence of the decomposition ($\\text{DAF} = \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$), not evidence of mechanism robustness.\n\n\\section{Cross-Model Verification}\n\\label{app:cross-model}\n\nTo test whether the death-avoidance finding generalizes beyond Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, we replicate the counterfactual analysis with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on 7 task types ($N{=}70$ episode pairs, 10 episodes per task). The results are strongly consistent with the Qwen findings: 24 episodes (34.3\\%) are deterministic-preferred, \\textbf{0} (0\\%) are internal-preferred, and 46 (65.7\\%) show no preference. The internal strategy dies in 58.6\\% of episodes versus 24.3\\% for deterministic (death-rate differential $+34.3\\%$), compared to $+26.6\\%$ for Qwen2.5-7B. Direction consistency is perfect: all 7 task types show higher internal than deterministic death rates (7/7, compared to 10/11 for Qwen). The larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.6\\%$) and the absence of any internal-preferred episodes (0 vs.\\ 9 for Qwen) suggest that Llama-3.1-8B is more vulnerable to hallucination-induced death, amplifying the death-avoidance effect. These results confirm that the death-avoidance dominance is not specific to the Qwen model family.\n\n\\section{Failure Mode Analysis}\n\\label{app:failure-modes}\n\nThe most common failure mode for the internal strategy is state hallucination: the model predicts that an action succeeded (e.g., ``the water is now boiling'') when the environment state did not change, leading to cascading errors that trigger agent death. The deterministic strategy avoids this by receiving ground-truth observations, but still dies in 16.3\\% of episodes, primarily from selecting inherently dangerous actions (e.g., eating toxic substances) rather than from state misestimation.\n\n\\section{Ablation Studies}\n\\label{app:ablations}\n\n\\paragraph{Capacity scaling.} We scale the implicit router from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B and evaluate at both step and episode level (Table~\\ref{tab:capacity}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Step Bal.\\ Acc.\\ (\\%) & Ep.\\ Bal.\\ Acc.\\ (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-0.6B & $56.4 \\pm 3.5$ & $50.7 \\pm 2.6$ \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B & $58.2 \\pm 4.0$ & $43.1 \\pm 1.0$ \\\\\nQwen3-4B & $59.2 \\pm 3.5$ & $\\mathbf{59.0 \\pm 1.9}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-det & 50.0 & 50.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Capacity scaling: step-level and episode-level balanced accuracy (4 seeds, identical configuration across all scales). Step-level accuracy is flat ($56$--$59\\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B breaks the random baseline ($59.0\\%$, $p < 0.005$).}\n\\label{tab:capacity}\n\\end{table}\n\nStep-level balanced accuracy is flat across scales ($56$--$59\\%$, within seed variance), indicating step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, only 4B breaks the random baseline ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The dominant explanation is that death avoidance constitutes a task-type-level binary signal that a step-level router cannot exploit efficiently.\n\n\\paragraph{Reasoning depth.} An ablation comparing chain-of-thought reasoning against direct-answer generation on 6 task types ($n{=}120$ episodes per condition, 6 tasks $\\times$ 20 episodes per task) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, $|d| \\leq 0.31$) and near-identical death rates (41.8\\% vs.\\ 42.5\\%); reasoning depth does not meaningfully alter internal-mode outcomes.\n\n\\section{Threshold Routing Analysis}\n\\label{sec:threshold-routing}\n\nGiven per-task death risk knowledge, we test whether a simple threshold routing strategy can outperform always-deterministic. For each threshold $t \\in [0, 1]$, we route to internal if the per-task internal death rate $\\leq t$, otherwise to deterministic, and sweep all thresholds to find the optimal.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Mean Score & Death Rate & $\\Delta$ vs Det \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-det & $-9.47$ & $0.163$ & $0.00$ \\\\\nOptimal threshold ($t{=}0$) & $-11.03$ & $0.155$ & $-1.56$ \\\\\nAlways-internal & $-39.07$ & $0.429$ & $-29.60$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Death-risk threshold routing analysis on 245 episodes. Bootstrap 95\\% CI for the difference: $[-8.87, +5.92]$.}\n\\label{tab:threshold-routing}\n\\end{table}\n\nEven with perfect per-task death risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform always-deterministic. The sole zero-death task achieves higher scores under deterministic (+28.5) than internal (+18.9), leaving no room for a beneficial mixed strategy.\n\n\\end{document}\n",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
  "old_string": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-environment evidence.}\nThe framework generates testable predictions beyond ScienceWorld. The MATH null ($p = 0.81$) validates the prediction that removing catastrophic failure eliminates routing signal; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-env}). True quantitative DAF replication requires a second catastrophic-failure environment (e.g., NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020}, Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}).\n\n\\paragraph{Penalty sensitivity and correlation structure.}\nThe $r = 0.97$ correlation is specific to catastrophic death ($P{=}100$); defining failure as any non-positive score yields $r = -0.13$ ($p = 0.70$). As shown in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}, this correlation follows algebraically from death dominance when $P$ is constant; larger models with lower death rates would reduce DAF. This prediction assumes models above a minimum capability threshold; preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests very weak models may exhibit low death rates not from avoidance ability but from inability to reach dangerous states.\n\n\\paragraph{Implications for router design.}\nDeath avoidance dominance (78.9\\% of non-tie labels, DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$) reframes modality routing as a \\emph{safety} problem, closer to a conservative default policy than to cost-quality optimization~\\citep{garcia2015comprehensive, cogrouter-2026}. The underlying failure mechanism is state hallucination: the internal strategy predicts that an action succeeded when the environment state did not change, and these prediction errors compound across steps until they trigger death. The deterministic strategy eliminates these compounding observation errors, while action-selection errors (16.3\\% deterministic death rate) remain comparatively rare. The 86:9 label imbalance explains why always-deterministic is a strong baseline; 4B parameters for $+9$\\,pp router improvement is consistent with the analytical framework being more efficient. This dominance is so thorough that even an oracle routing strategy with perfect per-task death risk knowledge cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}, Table~\\ref{tab:threshold-routing}).\n\n\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe framework applies most directly to environments with sparse terminal rewards and catastrophic failure modes. Environments with graded failure costs would yield intermediate DAF values; stronger backbones with lower hallucination rates would reduce death-rate differentials and consequently lower DAF.",
  "new_string": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch between routing signal and learning granularity. Death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property: a given task type either carries substantial death risk (10 of 11 types) or does not (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}). Step-level features (action text, observation content) correlate weakly with this task-type-level signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's modest episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. The per-task evaluation (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}) corroborates this: balanced accuracy is undefined for 7 of 11 task types (all-deterministic labels), and the remaining 4 types show no evidence of cross-type transfer.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}\nOur framework generates a testable prediction: stronger backbones will lower the death-avoidance fraction as capability reduces catastrophic failure rates. The Llama pilot ($\\delta = +34.3\\%$) versus Qwen ($\\delta = +26.6\\%$) comparison is consistent with this prediction in the capability-increase direction (Llama's higher death rate reflects weaker task competence, not stronger capability). For 70B-class models, we predict reduced $\\delta$ and consequently lower DAF, with the routing problem shifting from safety-dominated to quality-dominated. This prediction has a lower bound: preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests very weak models exhibit low death rates not from avoidance ability but from inability to reach dangerous states. The relationship between model capability and death rate is thus non-monotonic, with a peak at intermediate capability levels where models are competent enough to reach dangerous states but not robust enough to avoid them.\n\n\\paragraph{Self-correction as testable prediction.}\nThe death-avoidance mechanism makes a specific prediction about self-correction methods such as Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}: if self-correction reduces internal-strategy death rates without proportionally reducing task success, DAF should decrease as $\\delta$ shrinks. This is distinct from the capability-scaling prediction because self-correction targets the specific failure mode (compounding state hallucination) rather than general capability. Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023} represents a complementary intervention: by exploring alternative action sequences, it may recover from hallucination-induced errors before they cascade to death. Both predictions are testable within the existing framework by substituting the internal-strategy implementation while holding the environment and deterministic strategy constant.\n\n\\paragraph{DAF as penalty-dependent diagnostic.}\nThe DAF metric is a derived quantity: given $\\delta$, $P$, and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, DAF is determined ($\\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$). Its value increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}), a mathematical property rather than an empirical finding. The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction (78.9\\%) is therefore more informative as a primary diagnostic: it counts how many routing preferences trace to one strategy dying while the other survives, regardless of how death is penalized. The $r = 0.97$ per-task correlation (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}) is similarly a mathematical consequence: when $P$ is constant across task types and DAF $\\rightarrow 1$, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx P \\cdot \\delta$ makes $r \\rightarrow 1$ by construction. We retain both DAF and $r$ as consistency checks rather than independent findings. At $P \\leq 25$, the correlation drops below significance (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that death-signal dominance requires a penalty magnitude sufficient to overwhelm non-death score differences.\n\n\\paragraph{Framework applicability and boundary conditions.}\nThe framework requires three conditions: (1) paired episode collection under two modalities with identical initial conditions, (2) binary catastrophic outcomes (death/survival) distinguishable from graded performance, and (3) sparse enough rewards that episode-level comparison is necessary (dense rewards permit step-level labeling). ScienceWorld satisfies all three. MATH satisfies (1) but lacks (2) and (3), serving as a negative control; ALFWorld satisfies (1) and (3) but lacks (2), revealing that without catastrophic failure, the framework diagnoses alternative mechanisms (information asymmetry) rather than death avoidance. Environments with graded failure costs (e.g., partial penalties proportional to progress) would yield intermediate DAF values, and the framework could quantify how much of the routing signal traces to the most severe failure tier versus milder penalties. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, represent the most direct replication targets for quantitative DAF analysis."
}

The deterministic strategy eliminates these compounding observation errors, while action-selection errors (16.3\\% deterministic death rate) remain comparatively rare. The 86:9 label imbalance explains why always-deterministic is a strong baseline; 4B parameters for $+9$\\,pp router improvement is consistent with the analytical framework being more efficient. This dominance is so thorough that even an oracle routing strategy with perfect per-task death risk knowledge cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}, Table~\\ref{tab:threshold-routing}).",
          "+\\paragraph{Self-correction as testable prediction.}",
          "+The death-avoidance mechanism makes a specific prediction about self-correction methods such as Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}: if self-correction reduces internal-strategy death rates without proportionally reducing task success, DAF should decrease as $\\delta$ shrinks. This is distinct from the capability-scaling prediction because self-correction targets the specific failure mode (compounding state hallucination) rather than general capability. Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023} represents a complementary intervention: by exploring alternative action sequences, it may recover from hallucination-induced errors before they cascade to death. Both predictions are testable within the existing framework by substituting the internal-strategy implementation while holding the environment and deterministic strategy constant.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}",
          "-The framework applies most directly to environments with sparse terminal rewards and catastrophic failure modes. Environments with graded failure costs would yield intermediate DAF values; stronger backbones with lower hallucination rates would reduce death-rate differentials and consequently lower DAF.",
          "+\\paragraph{DAF as penalty-dependent diagnostic.}",
          "+The DAF metric is a derived quantity: given $\\delta$, $P$, and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, DAF is determined ($\\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$). Its value increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}), a mathematical property rather than an empirical finding. The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction (78.9\\%) is therefore more informative as a primary diagnostic: it counts how many routing preferences trace to one strategy dying while the other survives, regardless of how death is penalized. The $r = 0.97$ per-task correlation (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}) is similarly a mathematical consequence: when $P$ is constant across task types and DAF $\\rightarrow 1$, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx P \\cdot \\delta$ makes $r \\rightarrow 1$ by construction. We retain both DAF and $r$ as consistency checks rather than independent findings. At $P \\leq 25$, the correlation drops below significance (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that death-signal dominance requires a penalty magnitude sufficient to overwhelm non-death score differences.",
          "+",
          "+\\paragraph{Framework applicability and boundary conditions.}",
          "+The framework requires three conditions: (1) paired episode collection under two modalities with identical initial conditions, (2) binary catastrophic outcomes (death/survival) distinguishable from graded performance, and (3) sparse enough rewards that episode-level comparison is necessary (dense rewards permit step-level labeling). ScienceWorld satisfies all three. MATH satisfies (1) but lacks (2) and (3), serving as a negative control; ALFWorld satisfies (1) and (3) but lacks (2), revealing that without catastrophic failure, the framework diagnoses alternative mechanisms (information asymmetry) rather than death avoidance. Environments with graded failure costs (e.g., partial penalties proportional to progress) would yield intermediate DAF values, and the framework could quantify how much of the routing signal traces to the most severe failure tier versus milder penalties. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, represent the most direct replication targets for quantitative DAF analysis."
        ]
      }
    ],

  "old_string": "Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface additional signal. Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}; more sophisticated strategies (e.g., Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023}) may reduce internal death rates and lower DAF. Episode-level labels may assign suboptimal routing to individual steps, particularly those preceding death events, and training on pure-strategy trajectories introduces distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-env}, \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) provide supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; validation at larger scales remains future work.",
  "new_string": "Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface additional signal. Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}; more sophisticated strategies (e.g., Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023}) may reduce internal death rates and lower DAF. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, which may be suboptimal for individual steps (particularly those preceding death events); episodes within the same task type are not fully independent because they share environment dynamics, though they use different random seeds. The mislabeling fraction (episodes where the episode-level label disagrees with what step-level oracle labels would assign) is approximately 27.7\\%, concentrated in death-asymmetry episodes where early steps may have favored the eventually-dying strategy. Training on pure-strategy trajectories also introduces distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-env}, \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) provide preliminary supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. The Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct pilot (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; validation at larger scales remains future work."
