A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct replication (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns for all tasks, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; validation at larger scales remains future work.",
          "+Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface additional signal. Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}; more sophisticated strategies (e.g., Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023}) may reduce internal death rates and lower DAF. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, which may be suboptimal for individual steps (particularly those preceding death events); episodes within the same task type are not fully independent because they share environment dynamics, though they use different random seeds. The mislabeling fraction (episodes where the episode-level label disagrees with what step-level oracle labels would assign) is approximately 27.7\\%, concentrated in death-asymmetry episodes where early steps may have favored the eventually-dying strategy. Training on pure-strategy trajectories also introduces distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-env}, \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) provide preliminary supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. The Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct pilot (7 task types, $N{=}70$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}) confirms direction-consistent death patterns, though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; validation at larger scales remains future work.",
          " ",
          " \\section*{Ethics Statement}",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically across the environment (\S\ref{sec:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.
5	
6	\subsection{Problem Setup}
```

    "oldString": "Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically across the environment (\\S\\ref{sec:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.",
    "newString": "Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically across the environment (\\S\\ref{sec:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment $\\mathcal{E}$ with state space $\\mathcal{S}$, observation space $\\mathcal{O}$, and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent follows a policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ that maps observation histories to actions. At each step $t$, the agent receives observation $o_t$, selects action $a_t = \\pi(o_1, \\ldots, o_t)$, and the environment transitions to state $s_{t+1}$.\n\nWe define a modality set $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$ governing how the agent processes state transitions:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Internal} ($m = {\\tt int}$): The agent uses its own generation to predict intermediate observations. The LLM interleaves chain-of-thought reasoning with environment actions following the ReAct paradigm \\cite{react-2023}, but prediction errors compound across steps.\n    \\item \\textbf{Deterministic} ($m = {\\tt det}$): The agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step, equivalent to access to a perfect dynamics model. Action selection remains with the LLM; only the state-transition prediction is replaced.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent We restrict $\\mathcal{M}$ to two modalities. A reasoning-depth ablation (chain-of-thought vs.\\ direct-answer, $n{=}120$, 6 task types) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, near-identical death rates); details in Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}.\n\nA \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which modality to use at each step. The goal of modality routing is to learn $\\rho$ that maximizes expected episode return. Our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn such a router.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality, yielding no routing signal. We instead perform episode-level counterfactual analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and modality $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the modality choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two modalities can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score to reflect the asymmetric cost of catastrophic failure in interactive environments. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. The full procedure is given in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, modalities $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nThe algorithm prioritizes survival over score: death asymmetry (lines 5--8) determines preference before score comparison (lines 11--14). Label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. The full gap decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation holds exactly when the surviving strategy scores zero in all death-asymmetry episodes; in our data, the residual is small ($29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$, or $0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nA complementary question is whether death-avoidance signal is driven by a few outlier task types or distributed systematically. We quantify this via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI) based on the Gini coefficient over per-task routing signal contributions (formal definition in Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). SCI $\\in [0, 1]$: values near 0 indicate uniform distribution (systematic phenomenon); values near 1 indicate concentration in a single type (isolated phenomenon). Results are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n",
